- **`pandas` is now a declared dependency.** Fourteen modules `import pandas as pd` directly while it only ever arrived transitively through seaborn and anndata, which is precisely what let pip choose a different pandas *major* per Python minor without anything in this repository saying so. Declared as `pandas>=2.0`, with the floor commented as conservative rather than measured — the suite is exercised against 2.3, and against pandas-3 semantics only via `future.infer_string`.
- **`anndata` was checked and deliberately left alone.** Under `future.infer_string` this environment shows 24 further failures, but all of them are in three h5ad modules and all of them fail *inside* anndata 0.11.4's writer registry (`IORegistryError: No method registered for writing ArrowStringArrayNumpySemantics`), not in UELer. They never appeared in CI, and the combination is not reachable by a fresh install: pandas 3 needs ≥ 3.11, on 3.11+ anndata 0.12+ is available and handles it, and on 3.10 the newest anndata is 0.11.4 but pandas 3 cannot be installed at all. The floor stays `>=0.10`. Worth knowing for later, though: anndata 0.12 requires ≥ 3.11 and 0.13 requires ≥ 3.12, so the version of this repo's Python range under real pressure is the **3.10 floor**, not the 3.12 ceiling.
- **Nine new tests in `tests/test_cell_table_dtype_predicates.py`**, and they were checked for being vacuous: restoring the old `np.issubdtype` call makes the end-to-end group fail with 2 errors, and the new code makes it pass. They cover each dtype family, assert that `np.issubdtype` *does* raise on the extension dtypes (so the helper's reason for existing is written down as a test), and drive `apply_colors_to_masks` end to end through `category` and nullable-`Int64` identifier columns — cases that fail on **every** supported pandas, so the regression is caught without needing pandas 3. The pandas-3 string dtype is added to the parameter list only when constructible rather than guarded by `skipUnless`, because CI runs with `--max-skips 0`.
- Verified: `python tools/run_test_suite.py --max-skips 0` → **922 tests, OK**, "No tests were skipped", exit 0. Under `future.infer_string` the `StringDtype` errors go **19 → 0**, and the only remaining failures are the 24 anndata ones described above. `python -m build` clean and the wheel `METADATA` reads `Requires-Dist: pandas>=2.0`. **Not verified:** the 3.12 leg itself — that needs the next push.
- Recommendation left open for the developer: re-read the 3.12 leg after this lands, and note that the reading now argues for *widening* rather than narrowing. Either way the leg is worth keeping — it is currently the project's only coverage of pandas-3 semantics, and it is what caught this. A cheap standing reproducer, no new tooling required: run the suite under `python -c "import pandas as pd; pd.options.future.infer_string = True; import runpy, sys; sys.argv = ['x', '--max-skips', '0']; runpy.run_path('tools/run_test_suite.py', run_name='__main__')"`.

**Gate C of the PyPI release plan — CI, and a release mechanism that cannot fire by accident (issue #79)**
- Follows Gate A (`88b0e6e`) and Gate B (`f9fcfae`) in [`dev_note/issue_tracking/issue79_pypi_release_readiness.md`](../dev_note/issue_tracking/issue79_pypi_release_readiness.md). Gates A and B made the artifact correct and made it describe itself; Gate C is the machinery that keeps it that way and the machinery that publishes it. Until now UELer had exactly one workflow — `docs.yml` — so every release was validated on one developer machine, on one Python version, in an environment that happened to be missing bokeh.
- **`.github/workflows/tests.yml`, two jobs.** `unit` is a matrix over Python **3.10** and **3.11** that installs the full runtime stack and runs the suite; `package` builds the sdist and wheel, runs `twine check --strict`, cross-checks every version declaration, then installs the wheel into a clean virtualenv and imports it **from outside the repository** — the clean-checkout case a developer never sees locally, where an asset that `.gitignore` swallows is present in a local build and missing from a CI one. `package` uploads its artifacts, and the release workflow publishes exactly those, so what reaches an index is what was tested.
- **A skipped test is now a failure.** New `tools/run_test_suite.py` runs the same discovery `unittest` does, prints every skipped test **with its reason**, and exits non-zero if the count exceeds `--max-skips` (CI passes `0`; `make test-ci` runs the identical gate locally). This is aimed at a specific failure this project already had: plain `unittest` prints `OK` for a run that quietly dropped ~14 bokeh-gated tests, which is how the Python 3.11 coverage gap stayed invisible until the release audit found it. A complete environment skips **0 of 913**, so zero is a measured fact rather than a hopeful threshold. `actions/setup-node` is in the workflow for the same reason — two tests parse the anywidget ESM bundle with node and would otherwise skip silently.
- **The planned "CI fast-stub job" turned out not to be buildable, and that is worth recording.** The assumption — inherited from the packaging migration — was that `tests/bootstrap.py`'s stubs let the suite run without the heavy dependency stack. Measured in fresh virtualenvs: with numpy only, **302** of 913 tests are even collected and 113 error; adding `traitlets`, `anndata` and real `matplotlib` gets to **671** collected with 68 errors. The cause is structural: `_ensure_matplotlib_stub()` skips itself only when `matplotlib.pyplot` is **already in `sys.modules`**, which in a full environment happens because `seaborn_image` imports it first. In a minimal environment the stub does install, and it has no `matplotlib.path` and no `colors.Normalize`, so ~52 tests error out. So the stubs are a speed optimisation for an environment that is already complete, not a substitute for one — and a stub-based CI job would have shown a green tick over a third of the suite never running, which is worse than having no job. The long-standing open item is closed as **superseded**; the full install is ~15 s of wheels anyway.
- **Python 3.12 is now measured instead of argued about.** `requires-python = ">=3.10,<3.13"` permits 3.12, and nothing had ever run there. Rather than guess, the matrix carries a 3.12 leg marked `continue-on-error`, so the next push produces the evidence at no risk to the build. Acting on it is a one-line follow-up either way — add `Programming Language :: Python :: 3.12`, or tighten the bound to `<3.12`. The classifiers and `requires-python` must always move together.
- **`.github/workflows/release.yml` uses PyPI Trusted Publishing (OIDC) — no API token exists in the repository's secrets** — and is deliberately asymmetric: **pushing a `v*` tag gets you as far as TestPyPI and no further.** Reaching real PyPI requires an explicit `workflow_dispatch` with `publish_to: pypi` *and* a tag as the ref, behind a GitHub environment reviewer. The plan had tags publishing straight to PyPI; given that a version number there can be yanked but never replaced or reused, making `git push --tags` an irreversible public act seemed like the wrong default for the one operation in this project that cannot be undone.
- **New `tools/check_release_tag.py` compares four things, not two:** the tag, `pyproject.toml`, `ueler.__version__`, and both `dist/` filenames — so a release cannot ship with the runtime version disagreeing with the artifact. The comparison is on **PEP 440-normalised** versions, which is load-bearing rather than pedantic: this repo tags in SemVer style (`v0.2.0-alpha` is an existing tag) while setuptools writes `0.5.0a0`, so a string comparison would reject a *correct* tag. `v0.5.0-alpha` and `v0.5.0-a0` both validate. Run it before tagging with `make check-release TAG=v0.5.0-alpha`.
- **The `v0.4.2`–`v0.4.4` tags are not being backfilled.** The plan read those gaps as drift; they were intentional. `doc/log.md` remains the record of what shipped, and tags now mark what was released *through the release workflow* — which did not exist then. No consequence for the workflow: it triggers on tag pushes, and the historical tags are already pushed, so the first tag it will ever see is the next one. That will be **`v0.5.0-alpha`**, created after Gate D.
- **A blocker turned up on the way in, and it is the same bug Gate A fixed for assets.** `.gitignore` ignored `.github/` as a blanket *directory* rule, so both new workflow files were invisible to `git status` — they would have been committed nowhere and run never, and the whole gate would have looked done while doing nothing. It also explains why `docs.yml` was the only tracked file under `.github/`: it had been force-added. Gate A's own technique note predicted the trap — a `!` negation cannot re-include anything beneath an ignored directory, so `!.github/workflows/*.yml` would not have helped. The rule is now narrowed to the local-only assistant scaffolding (`.github/agents/`, `.github/chatmodes/`, `.github/prompts/`, `.github/copilot-instructions.md`), leaving `.github/workflows/` tracked. Worth remembering as a pattern: a blanket directory rule in this repo's `.gitignore` is a thing you have to *narrow*, never patch with a negation.
- Verified: both workflow files parse as YAML and resolve to the job graph `tests → verify → {testpypi, pypi}`; the Python embedded in the wheel-check step was extracted, `ast.parse`d, and then **run verbatim** against a clean-venv install of `dist/ueler-0.5.0a0-py3-none-any.whl` from `/tmp` — it imports `ueler`, imports the heaviest module `ueler.viewer.main_viewer`, reads the packaged `ready.png` through `load_asset_bytes`, and asserts that none of the four legacy top-level names leaked into `sys.modules` — exit 0. The skip gate was exercised in both directions (exit 0 with 0 skips on the full stack, exit 1 in a minimal venv with all 19 skips printed and named), and the tag checker against the real `dist/` for a correct tag in both spellings (exit 0) and a wrong one (exit 1). `make build` clean, `twine check --strict` PASSED on both artifacts, `python -m unittest discover tests` → **913 tests, OK**, `mkdocs build` exit 0. **Not verified:** nothing has run on GitHub's runners yet — the first push is the real test of the workflow files, and the publish jobs cannot be exercised without a tag.

**Relicensed from GPL-3.0-only to BSD-3-Clause, before the first PyPI upload (issue #79)**
- Closes the open decision left by Gate B's **B4**: that item documented the license UELer *had*, it did not settle whether that license was right. It was not. UELer is distributed as a package other people **import**, and that is where copyleft has the sharpest teeth — a user's analysis pipeline that imports `ueler` and is then distributed inherits the GPL. For a viewer whose purpose is to be embedded in other people's notebooks and pipelines, the license was working against the tool.
- **Two things were checked before recommending it, because both could have made the change impossible.** First, **who holds copyright**: `git shortlog -sne --all` shows one human — `yulewu <waltwu512@gmail.com>` (458 commits) and `Yu-Le Wu <…@users.noreply.github.com>` (19) are the same person; the only other authors are `copilot-swe-agent[bot]` (12) and `github-actions[bot]` (1), which commit on the repo owner's behalf. So no contributor round-up was needed, and this is the cheapest the change will ever be — every future contributor's work is licensed under whatever is in the tree when they submit it. Second, **whether anything obliged the GPL**: every runtime dependency is permissive (BSD-3 for scikit-image, dask, bokeh, panel, ipywidgets, anndata, tifffile, fsspec, ipympl; MIT for anywidget, watchfiles, seaborn-image; Apache-2.0 for bqplot, jupyter-scatter, requests, and `ark-analysis` in the `[ark]` extra), so the GPL was a standalone choice rather than something inherited and unremovable.
- **Why BSD-3 rather than a weaker copyleft.** The scientific Python neighbourhood is BSD-3 almost uniformly — scikit-image, dask, bokeh, panel, anndata, scanpy, and **napari**, which UELer's own `keywords` names as the comparison point. MPL-2.0 was the considered alternative (file-level copyleft: modify UELer's files and publish those changes, but importing it carries no obligation) and remains the right answer if protection against closed forks ever becomes a goal. LGPL was rejected specifically for Python: its obligations are written around "linking", which maps badly onto `import`, and the resulting ambiguity makes risk-averse organisations avoid LGPL as readily as GPL — most of the cost for murkier protection. Also worth stating plainly: the GPL was never the mechanism for getting cited. Attribution comes from BSD-3's notice requirement plus a citation file and a paper.
- **`LICENSE.txt` replaced** with the BSD-3-Clause text. Note what the old file did *not* contain: the stock 674-line GPL text carried only the FSF's notice on the license document itself and **no copyright line for UELer at all**, so the project had never actually asserted its own copyright. The new file does: `Copyright (c) 2025-2026, Yu-Le Wu, Hartmann Lab, German Cancer Research Center (DKFZ), Heidelberg, Germany` (2025 from the first commit, 2025-10-15).
- **Also updated:** `pyproject.toml` (`license = "BSD-3-Clause"` → `License-Expression: BSD-3-Clause` in the metadata), the README License section, and `docs/index.md`. Still no `License ::` classifier — PEP 639 forbids combining one with the SPDX expression, which is unchanged by the relicense.
- **One thing left for the developer, and it is not a code change:** confirm with DKFZ that naming both the author and the institute in the copyright line matches institutional policy. As a Helmholtz centre DKFZ may have rules on how lab software is licensed, and that constraint outranks the technical argument above.
- Scope note: the relicense applies going forward. Tags up to `v0.4.1` remain GPL-3.0 as published, which is normal and harmless — nothing was ever uploaded to PyPI under the GPL, so there is no released artifact to reconcile. Existing users only gain permissions.
- Verified: `python -m build` clean, `twine check --strict` PASSED on both artifacts, wheel `METADATA` reads `License-Expression: BSD-3-Clause` with `License-File: LICENSE.txt`, the BSD text is present in `dist-info/licenses/`, and the rewritten README section renders in the long description. `git grep -i gpl` over the tracked tree returns only the historical entries in this log and in `dev_note/`. `python -m unittest discover tests` → **913 tests, OK**.

**Gate B of the PyPI release plan — the release now describes itself correctly (issue #79)**
- Follows Gate A (`88b0e6e`) in [`dev_note/issue_tracking/issue79_pypi_release_readiness.md`](../dev_note/issue_tracking/issue79_pypi_release_readiness.md). Where Gate A was about the *artifact* being correct, Gate B is about everything a stranger reads before deciding to install: the PyPI sidebar, the license, and the install instructions on the docs site. Nothing here can break a build, which is exactly why it is easy to ship a first release without it.
- **PyPI metadata filled out.** `classifiers` went from two entries to ten: `Development Status :: 4 - Beta`, `Intended Audience :: Science/Research`, `Framework :: Jupyter`, the three `Topic :: Scientific/Engineering ::` entries (Bio-Informatics, Visualization, Image Processing), and the per-minor `Python :: 3.10` / `3.11` classifiers that make the range agreed in Gate A visible on the page rather than only in `Requires-Python`. Deliberately **no** `License ::` classifier — PEP 639 forbids combining one with the `license` SPDX expression, and setuptools warns; a comment in `pyproject.toml` records that so it does not get "fixed" later. `[project.urls]` gained `Repository`, `Issues` and `Changelog`, and the two existing keys were capitalised to match the ecosystem convention for the sidebar labels.
- **The license is now stated where users look, not only in the repo tree.** `README.md` gained a "License" section naming **GPL-3.0-only** with one plain-language paragraph on what copyleft does and does not require — specifically that analysing your own data with UELer places no obligation on that data or on your results, which is the question an evaluator in a clinical or commercial setting actually has. A short "Issues and contact" section was added alongside. `docs/index.md` already carried a License section, so only the README half was missing.
- **The docs site no longer contradicts the README.** `docs/installation.md` was still clone-only — env → `git clone` → `pip install -e .` — so a reader arriving from a PyPI page would have been told to clone anyway. It now opens with "Option A — install from PyPI" (the `--pre` form, the `[ark]`/`[docs]` extras, `pip install --upgrade`, and a pointer to the starter notebook, which is *not* part of the package) before the source path, and "Requirements" says 3.10 or 3.11 instead of the stale open-ended `≥ 3.10`. "Updating UELer" covers both install paths, and the development section documents the opt-in test bootstrap and the `build`/`twine` additions to the `dev` extra. This gap was not in the original Gate B list; it was found while checking B4.
- **`ueler/graphify-out/` moved out of the package directory** to `graphify-out/legacy-ueler-scoped-2026-07-31/`. It was a stale, gitignored graph scoped to `ueler/` (2026-07-31), superseded by the repo-root graph, and verified absent from both artifacts — so this is hygiene rather than a bug fix. The reason to do it anyway: 11 MB of generated JSON sitting inside the distributed package directory is one `package-data` glob away from shipping, and the `.gitignore` negation technique used in Gate A cannot help, because a directory rule beats any file-level re-inclusion. `graphify query` still resolves from the new location. The relocated copy is regenerable and safe to delete.
- **Two Gate B items were consciously declined rather than done.** `ipykernel` and `ipympl` stay hard runtime dependencies for `0.5.0`: moving them to a `notebook` extra would also require changing [`.binder/postBuild`](../.binder/postBuild) in the same commit (it runs a bare `pip install .` and depends on those two arriving via core deps), and there is no non-notebook user to serve yet. No `console_scripts` entry point either — UELer is notebook-first and `ueler.runner.run_viewer` is the programmatic entry, so a `ueler` command would add a support surface for little gain. Both are recorded in the plan so the omissions read as decisions.
- Verified against artifacts: `python -m build` clean, `twine check --strict` **PASSED** on both (which is also what validates the ten classifiers against the trove list), wheel `METADATA` shows all ten classifiers, the five `Project-URL` entries, `Metadata-Version: 2.4`, `License-Expression: GPL-3.0-only` and `Requires-Python: <3.13,>=3.10`, and no path containing `graphify` appears in either the wheel or the sdist. `mkdocs build` exits 0. `python -m unittest discover tests` → **913 tests, OK**, unchanged.

**Gate A of the PyPI release plan — the build is now safe to publish (issue #79)**
- Follow-up to the release-readiness plan in [`dev_note/issue_tracking/issue79_pypi_release_readiness.md`](../dev_note/issue_tracking/issue79_pypi_release_readiness.md). Gate A is the set of items that would have made the *first* PyPI upload wrong rather than merely improvable, and the reason it is a gate at all is that **PyPI is append-only**: a version number can be yanked but never reused, so a bad first upload is permanent public record. All eight items are done; Gates B–D (metadata polish, CI, TestPyPI rehearsal) remain open.
- **The `.gitignore` finding was the one that could have shipped a broken wheel to users only.** Blanket `*.txt` and `*.png` rules meant `LICENSE.txt` and `ueler/viewer/images/ready.png` were in the repo only because someone had used `git add -f`; a **new** packaged asset was silently ignored. Because setuptools builds from the *working tree*, the developer's own build would have contained that asset and a CI build from a clean checkout would not — a `FileNotFoundError` in `load_asset_bytes()` that is invisible to the person who introduced it. Fixed with negation patterns placed *below* the blanket rules (last match wins, so the order is load-bearing): `!LICENSE.txt`, `!ueler/**/*.png`, `!doc/**/*.png`, `!docs/**/*.png`. `docs/` was added because the mkdocs site images sat in the same trap. Verified with probe files: new assets under `ueler/viewer/images/` and `doc/` now appear in `git status`, while `dev_note/*.png` and root `*.txt` scratch files stay ignored. Note the technique's limit — negation cannot re-include anything under an ignored *directory*, so it does nothing for `graphify-out/`.
- **The test bootstrap is now opt-in (`UELER_TEST_BOOTSTRAP=1`) instead of opt-out.** `sitecustomize.py` / `usercustomize.py` at the repo root auto-imported `tests.bootstrap`, which replaces `pandas`, `matplotlib`, `ipywidgets` and friends with stubs, and swallowed every failure in a bare `except Exception`. Measuring it shrank the problem and clarified the fix: the repo's `sitecustomize.py` is **never imported** for plain `python -c` / `python -m` (the cwd entry reaches `sys.path` after `site` has run), so `PYTHONPATH=<repo>` was effectively the only trigger; and in a *complete* environment the stubs are near-inert anyway, because each `_ensure_*` helper installs a stub only when the real module is missing. Inverted regardless — a debugging trap that only fires in incomplete environments is precisely the one that wastes a day. `UELER_SKIP_TEST_BOOTSTRAP=1` is still honoured as a hard override, the bare `except` now emits a `RuntimeWarning` naming the failure so a *requested* bootstrap cannot fail silently, and `make test-fast` / `test-integration` set the new variable. The suite never depended on the hook: `tests/__init__.py` imports `tests.bootstrap`, which calls `initialize()` at module scope — 913 tests pass identically with the variable set, unset, and under `PYTHONPATH=.`.
- **`MANIFEST.in` added, and it fixes a real inconsistency rather than just trimming size.** Default sdist rules pulled in 60+ `tests/test_*.py` but *not* `tests/__init__.py` or `tests/bootstrap.py`, so the shipped tests could not even be imported. Shipping none is the honest choice: making them runnable means shipping the stub machinery, which is dev-only. The sdist is now 77 files — `ueler/`, `ueler.egg-info/`, `PKG-INFO`, `LICENSE.txt`, `README.md`, `pyproject.toml`, `setup.cfg`, `MANIFEST.in` — and the wheel 64, with no strays outside `ueler/` and `*.dist-info/`. The trade-off to remember: a downstream packager (conda-forge, Debian) can no longer run the suite from the sdist.
- **`build-system.requires` raised to `setuptools>=77`** and `wheel` dropped (unnecessary since setuptools 70.1). The `license = "GPL-3.0-only"` expression and `license-files` are PEP 639 features that need 77; the previous `>=68` floor built correctly here only because the isolated build environment happened to fetch 80.9, and a pinned, offline, or `--no-build-isolation` build could have silently dropped the license metadata. `METADATA` now confirms `Metadata-Version: 2.4`, `License-Expression: GPL-3.0-only`, `License-File: LICENSE.txt`.
- **Release targets added to the `Makefile`:** `clean-dist`, `build` (depends on `clean-dist`), `check-dist` (`twine check --strict`), `publish-test`, `publish`. `publish*` depend on `check-dist` rather than `build`, deliberately — so an upload sends exactly the artifacts that were built and inspected instead of silently rebuilding them. `build>=1.0` and `twine>=5.0` joined the `dev` extra. The stale `ueler-0.3.1` artifacts in `dist/` (developer test output) were purged; the point of `clean-dist` is that `twine upload dist/*` globs whatever is present.
- **One name for the tool, in all four places.** `description`, the README title, `mkdocs.yml` and `docs/index.md` had drifted into three different expansions of the acronym. Settled on **"Unified Exploratory Linked Viewer"** scoped to *spatial proteomics and multiplexed imaging*; `pyproject.toml` and `docs/index.md` were the stragglers still reading "Usability Enhanced … MIBI". `keywords` broadened accordingly (MIBI kept as a search term rather than the framing). This matters on day one because `description` becomes the PyPI one-line summary and the README becomes the long description verbatim — both awkward to walk back.
- **The README is the PyPI landing page, so it was rewritten to work as one.** Installation now leads with `pip install ueler` (plus the `--pre` form while the version is a pre-release, the `[ark]`/`[docs]` extras, and the supported Python versions), with the clone + editable flow retitled as the development option; "Upgrade UELer" no longer tells pip users to `git pull`; "Getting started" no longer assumes a clone. Separately, every repo-relative link was made absolute — `![GUI_preview](/doc/GUI_preview.png)` and both `doc/log.md` links render on GitHub but **break on PyPI**, where there is no repository root.
- **`requires-python` narrowed to `">=3.10,<3.13"`.** The open-ended `>=3.10` promised 3.12 and 3.13, which have never been run; pip would have installed happily and failed somewhere unreproducible. Honest bound now, widened once CI proves otherwise. Residual gap recorded rather than glossed: `<3.13` still permits **3.12**, so either 3.12 joins the CI matrix or the bound tightens to `<3.12`.
- Verified as a whole, against artifacts rather than config: `python -m build` clean, `twine check --strict` PASSED on both, wheel **and** sdist each installed into their own fresh venv and imported from outside the repo (so the source tree could not shadow the installed package), bundled assets read back at the right byte counts (`loading.gif` 6561, `ready.png` 1202), `import ueler` still adds nothing to `sys.meta_path` and claims none of the four legacy top-level names, and `git archive HEAD ueler/viewer/images` confirms a clean checkout carries all three asset files. `python -m unittest discover tests` → **913 tests, OK**, unchanged from the pre-change baseline.

**Packaging prerequisite — `import ueler` no longer claims top-level module names (pre-PyPI cleanup)**
- Found while assessing the repo for a PyPI upload, not reported as a bug: `import ueler` had a **process-wide side effect**. `ueler/__init__.py` eagerly called `_register_module_aliases(UTILITY_ALIASES)` and `_register_package_prefixes(LEGACY_PACKAGE_PREFIXES)`, each of which inserted a `MetaPathFinder` at **`sys.meta_path[0]`** — ahead of the standard `PathFinder` — claiming the top-level names `viewer`, `constants`, `data_loader` and `image_utils` for the pre-0.2 notebook layout. Harmless in a private repo that nobody imports alongside anything else; unacceptable in a pip-installable package, where `constants` and `data_loader` are about the most collision-prone module names a user's own notebook could define.
- **The existing guard was not sufficient, because it was registration-time only.** `register_package_prefixes()` skipped a prefix whose real spec was already findable (`importlib.util.find_spec(prefix) is not None`), so a `viewer` package installed *before* UELer was left alone. But the finder stayed installed and unconditional afterwards: anything that became importable **after** `import ueler` — a later `sys.path` insert, a changed working directory, a notebook-local `constants.py` — was still shadowed, silently and with no diagnostic. Precedence is the whole point of `meta_path[0]`, and it cannot be made conditional after the fact.
- **Fix: the compatibility layer was deleted rather than made opt-in.** `ueler/_compat.py` (295 lines: `_AliasModuleFinder`, `_PrefixAliasFinder`, `UTILITY_ALIASES`, `LEGACY_PACKAGE_PREFIXES`, `LEGACY_VIEWER_ALIASES`, `LEGACY_PLUGIN_ALIASES`, `SHIM_ALIAS_MAP`, `register_module_aliases`, `register_package_prefixes`, `ensure_aliases_loaded`) is gone, as is the public `ueler.ensure_compat_aliases()` and its `__all__` entry. An env-gated opt-in was considered and rejected: the layer had no remaining consumer. `git grep` over every tracked `.py`/`.ipynb` found **zero** legacy imports outside the shim's own test — all three shipped notebooks (`script/run_ueler.ipynb`, `run_ueler_BIA.ipynb`, `run_ueler_binder.ipynb`) already `import ueler` — so keeping a dormant hijack of four generic names to serve nothing was not worth the ecosystem risk. Confirmed with the developer that pre-0.2 notebooks need no support.
- **`run_viewer(ensure_aliases=...)` and `run_viewer_bia(ensure_aliases=...)` are removed, but not from the signature outward only.** Both functions forward `**viewer_kwargs` verbatim to the viewer factory, so simply deleting the parameter would have let an old call's `ensure_aliases=False` fall through to `ImageMaskViewer(...)` and surface as an opaque `TypeError` naming a keyword the user never heard of. A shared `_drop_removed_kwargs()` pops it and raises a `DeprecationWarning` that says where to go instead (`stacklevel=3`, so the warning points at the caller's notebook cell).
- Note that this removes the *aliases*, not the modules: `ueler.constants`, `ueler.data_loader`, `ueler.image_utils` and `ueler.viewer.*` are untouched and remain the canonical import paths. The only breakage is code that imported them under their bare legacy names, which nothing in the repo does.
- Verified against a real installed wheel, not just the working tree: rebuilt, `pip install --force-reinstall` into a clean venv, and confirmed `import ueler` adds **no** entries to `sys.meta_path`, that `viewer`/`constants`/`data_loader`/`image_utils` resolve to nothing, that `import viewer.main_viewer` now fails with a plain `ModuleNotFoundError`, and that `_compat.py` is absent from the wheel while `ueler.viewer.main_viewer` and the bundled `images/` assets still load.
- Tests: `tests/test_shims_imports.py` (5 tests asserting the aliases resolved to the *same* module object) was deleted and replaced by `tests/test_import_namespace_hygiene.py` (5 tests) — the shims' absence is now itself a regression test. Three of them run `import ueler` in a **subprocess**, which is not optional: this suite's own bootstrap stubs modules and other test modules import `ueler`, so `sys.meta_path` in the parent process says nothing about what a fresh import does. They assert no finder is added, that none of the four names resolve into the `ueler` package, and that the legacy import fails cleanly. `tests/test_runner.py` and `tests/test_issue110_bia_loader.py` dropped their `patch("ueler.runner.ensure_aliases_loaded")` context managers, and `test_runner.py` gained coverage for the `DeprecationWarning` and for the removed kwarg **not** reaching the factory. `python -m unittest discover tests` → **913 tests, OK** (912 before). Not verified in a notebook: the dev environment has no browser, so the import contract is asserted through a clean-venv install rather than a live kernel.

### v0.4.4
**Issue #132 — Mask painter: fill and border are now independent, and the two coloring modes line up**
- Reported as a UX inconsistency between the discrete (categorical) and continuous painting modes. Three concrete problems: the `Identifier:` dropdown sat above the mode toggle in the shared header although `build_painter_state_maps_for_fov` ignores it entirely on the continuous branch; fill and border were entangled (`Global fill` + `Borders on filled` in discrete, a single `Fill (unchecked = outline)` multiplexer in continuous, which had no border control at all and returned an empty `border_map`); and there was no border opacity anywhere, nor a user-defined border color.
- **Fix, as requested in the issue: make `Fill` and `Border` orthogonal switches for every painted cell, in both modes.** Fill on + border on → filled with a border on top; fill on + border off → fill only; fill off + border on → an outline, which is what the old "unchecked fill" produced; fill off + border off → the cell is not painted, a state the old UI could not reach. `Fill (unchecked = outline)` therefore loses its parenthetical and `Global fill` is renamed `Fill all classes`, both keeping their own opacity input. The identifier moved into the categorical block, where it actually applies. The border group is deliberately *not* per-mode — a border checkbox, opacity and color mean the same thing however a cell got its color — so it renders once below both mode blocks and stays visible in each.
- **Border geometry is unchanged.** `_apply_region_colors()` still paints the two border groups the #131 rewrite established, with the clipping rules inherited from #91: an unfilled cell keeps the spillable outline (`_max_dilate_labels_4`), a filled one keeps the border clipped to its own pixels so it cannot dim a neighbour's fill (`_dilate_within_labels`). Only what those pixels are *colored*, their alpha, and whether they are drawn at all changed. Border opacity is a new scalar `border_alpha` that `_paint_border_group` blends with; at 1.0 the pixels are still written directly, so the opaque case is byte-for-byte what it was. The gallery path needed no new machinery — `engine._blend_mask_pixels` already honoured `MaskRenderSettings.alpha` for `mode="outline"`.
- **Border color is now a real control, so its old fallback had to go.** `border_map` used to be populated only for filled cells, with the overlay falling back to the class color for everything else; it now carries a color for *every* painted cell, including the continuous branch, and both overlay groups read it. The dropdown gained a third option: `Painted color` (default — the class color in discrete mode, the colormap color in continuous; the old `Same as fill`), `Mask color`, and `Custom…` with a color picker that is hidden unless selected. `_on_border_change` also clears the per-FOV state-map cache, which the old border handler did not: without that a new border color would not reach the canvas until some unrelated edit happened to invalidate it.
- **The border default is `Painted color`, not the `Mask color` the issue text asked for.** Once borders are orthogonal the default governs the outline of *unfilled* cells too, which had always drawn in their **class** color — so shipping `Mask color` would have made the out-of-the-box view (`Fill all classes` off, `Border` on) draw mask-colored outlines and hide the very colors the painter exists to show. Raised and confirmed with the developer before changing it. Note that `MaskPainterSnapshot.border_color_mode` and the `build_painter_state_maps_for_fov` parameter still default to `"mask_type_color"`: those are the *legacy* fallbacks for payloads written before the field existed, and are deliberately not the UI default. Palettes and ROI/checkpoint snapshots saved before this change stored `border_color_mode = "mask_type_color"` explicitly and are restored as saved, so they will draw mask-colored outlines until switched over.
- **Opacity inputs widened and unified.** All three (`global_fill_opacity_input`, the new `border_opacity_input`, and `continuous_opacity_input`) now share one `OPACITY_INPUT_WIDTH = "150px"`, up from 95px for the fill and border fields and 130px for the continuous one, so the discrete and continuous panes line up.
- Renames, deliberate rather than aliased, because the old names describe the old coupling: `apply_registry_colors(show_borders_on_filled=)` → `show_borders=` (plus `border_alpha=`), `MaskPainterSnapshot.show_borders_on_filled` → `show_borders` (default **True**, plus `border_opacity` and `border_custom_color`), and `show_fill_borders_checkbox` → `border_checkbox`. `resolve_mask_painter_snapshot_for_fov()` returns a 6-tuple. Snapshots, saved palettes and stored ROIs written before the split lack `show_borders` and are read as borders-**on**, since they always outlined unfilled cells.
- Tests: `tests/test_mask_color_overlay.py`'s `_reference_apply_region_colors()` oracle was carried forward to the new semantics — it now paints unfilled and filled cells as two passes and resolves overlapping owners *before* blending, so the parity tests still compare the vectorised overlay against a readable per-cell reference rather than hand-written expectations. New coverage for both switches off painting nothing, unfilled cells taking the border color, border-opacity blending, zero border opacity, the identifier's placement, the shared border group, the custom-color picker's visibility, continuous border colors under each mode, and the cache invalidation above. `python -m unittest discover -s tests -t .` → **912 tests, OK** (901 before). Not verified in a notebook: the dev environment has no browser, so the layout is asserted through the widget tree rather than seen. See `dev_note/issue_tracking/issue132_mask_painter_fill_border_ux.md`.

**Issue #131 — Mask painter outline rendering is no longer O(cells × pixels)**
- Reported as "rendering is much slower when `Fill (unchecked = outline)` is not checked". Measured through the real `apply_registry_colors()` on a 1024×1024 region: fill stayed at ~150 ms whatever the cell count, while outline cost 4.7 s at 200 cells, 23.6 s at 1000 and **116.9 s at 5000** — dead linear, ≈23 ms *per cell per render*. cProfile at 1000 cells put 20.7 s of 22.6 s (92%) inside `find_boundaries`, i.e. 2000 `scipy.ndimage.min_or_max_filter` calls at ~10 ms each.
- **Cause: the two modes were different algorithms, and the fast one failed closed.** `_can_vectorize_fill()` gated a vectorised LUT path that returned `False` if *any* coloured cell had `mode != "fill"`, so unchecking `Fill` for a single class dropped **every** cell in the FOV into the per-cell loop. That loop ran, per cell, a full-region `region_array == id`, two `np.any`, a `find_boundaries(mask_bool)` — a grey erosion *and* dilation across the whole region to outline one ~1000-px cell — an optional `thicken_outline`, and a full-region boolean-index write. It also held one full-region bool array **per cell** in `pending_edges` (~1 GB at 1000 cells × 1 MP, ~5 GB at 5000) and computed `np.unique(region_array)` twice.
- **Fix: batch the boundary computation, and delete the fork rather than add a third path.** `find_boundaries(labels, "inner")` is `(dilation(img) != erosion(img)) & (img != background)`, so restricting it to one cell gives exactly the pixels of that cell adjacent to a different label — identical to `find_boundaries(labels == cell, "inner")`, including at array borders and against excluded or uncoloured neighbours. One call now serves every cell. `ueler/rendering/engine.py` has always rendered whole-mask outlines this way; the painter overlay simply never adopted it. `_apply_region_colors()` is now a single O(pixels) pass for **all** modes: one `np.unique`, three lookup tables (fill, unclipped outline border, clipped filled border), one vectorised fill blend, one boundary pass, two border passes. Fills are still blended before any border is drawn, preserving the ordering fix from #91.
- **Thickening had to keep two distinct clipping rules,** both inherited from #91. Filled-cell borders are clipped to the owning cell so they cannot dim a neighbour's fill; outline-mode edges are deliberately *not* clipped and may spill. They get separate helpers. Outline borders dilate an *owner-label* image with `_max_dilate_labels_4()` — `np.maximum` in place of `|` keeps each thickened pixel attributed to a cell and reproduces the old rule that the largest mask id wins an overlap (the old `pending_edges` list was painted in ascending id order, last write winning). Confined borders use `_dilate_within_labels()`, which propagates only through same-label pixels: a free dilation followed by a clip lets a neighbour's higher id win ground the clip then discards, **erasing borders the per-cell version drew** — the parity tests caught precisely that. The two agree because the shortest path from a pixel to its own cell's boundary never leaves that cell (the step before it would exit is itself a boundary pixel, which would have ended the path sooner). Both helpers mirror `engine._binary_dilation_4` shift for shift, including its border handling.
- **Result: outline is now flat in cell count and no longer the slow mode.** At 5000 cells, 116.9 s → **65 ms** (~1800×); at 1000, 23.6 s → 40 ms. Outline now beats fill, which still blends across the whole region while outline touches only boundary pixels.
- One deliberate, bounded behaviour change: when a thick outline (`outline_thickness ≥ 2`) and a filled cell's border land on the same pixel, the filled border now wins, where the old result depended on which mask id happened to be larger. At the default thickness of 1 there is no dilation, edges never leave their own cell, and the output is bit-identical.
- Tests: `tests/test_mask_color_overlay.py` gained `VectorizedOverlayParityTests` (7 tests) built on `_reference_apply_region_colors()`, an executable copy of the pre-#131 per-cell loop — the new implementation is asserted equal to the algorithm it replaced for plain outlines, thick outlines, mixed modes with per-cell opacity and border colours, excluded and uncoloured ids, thick filled borders, and largest-id-wins overlaps. A separate test asserts `find_boundaries` is called exactly **once** per region at both 4 and 400 cells, which encodes the fix itself without depending on wall-clock timing. Four existing tests that patched `find_boundaries`/`thicken_outline` with per-cell fakes were rewritten against real geometry: they described the old call shape, not the behaviour, and their fake edge masks already matched what the real function returns. `python -m unittest discover -s tests -t .` → **901 tests, OK** (894 before). Not verified in a notebook: the dev environment has no browser, so the perceived interactive speed-up is inferred from the benchmark. See `dev_note/issue_tracking/issue131_outline_render_performance.md`.

**Issue #130 — The ROI manager's advanced filter now accepts tags containing spaces**
- The ROI manager has always accepted tags with spaces (`ROIManager._normalise_tags` only strips the ends of each comma-separated entry), but the advanced filter could not match them: typing `my tag` failed with `Expression did not reduce to a single value.`, and the helper button for such a tag inserted text the parser rejected.
- **Cause:** `tag_expression._tokenize()` flushed the name buffer on *every* whitespace character, so `my tag` lexed to two `name` tokens. `_to_postfix` passed both through and `_evaluate_postfix` finished with two values on the stack — hence the opaque error. Quoting (`'my tag'`) worked, but nothing told the user that, and the tag buttons do not quote what they insert.
- **Fix, as suggested in the issue:** whitespace now only separates a name from an operator, a parenthesis or a quote — never one name from another. `_tokenize` appends whitespace to the current buffer (skipping it while the buffer is empty, which drops leading spaces) and flushes only at those boundaries. Names are normalised with `" ".join(text.split())` — ends stripped, internal runs collapsed to one space — and the predicate normalises the ROI's tags the same way, so `my   tag` still matches the tag `my tag` and nothing that matched before stops matching. `tumour core & !necrotic edge` and `good roi & (figure 1 | figure 2)` now work unquoted.
- Quoted names are normalised identically, so `'my tag'` and `my tag` are equivalent; quoting stays useful for names holding operator characters (`'a&b'`). Two smaller repairs came with it: the quoted-string branch was missing a `flush_buffer()`, so `foo "bar"` emitted its tokens out of order, and an empty quoted name (`''`) silently produced a name that could never match — it now raises. Adjacent names, which only quoting can still produce, report `Missing operator between 'a' and 'b'.` instead of the old stack error.
- No change was needed in `roi_manager_plugin.py` or the anywidget editor: the tag helper buttons insert the raw tag text, which is now parseable as-is.
- Tests: new `TagExpressionSpacedNameTests` in `tests/test_tag_expression.py` (12 tests — bare spaced tags, spaced tags with `&`/`!`/`|`/parentheses, end-stripping, whitespace-run collapsing on both the expression and the tags, quoted/bare equivalence, operator characters inside quotes, and the three error paths). `python -m unittest discover -s tests -t .` → **894 tests, OK** (882 before). See `dev_note/issue_tracking/issue130_tag_expression_spaces.md`.

**Issue #127 reply — Switching the histogram's interaction mode no longer resets the plot**
- Reported after the multi-channel gating landed: switching between **Brush** and **Cutoff** remembers the selection but still resets the plot. Both halves of that are accurate. The gate survives because it lives in `_gates`, independent of any figure; the plot resets because `_on_interaction_mode_change()` called `self._render()`, which runs `_build_figures()` and swaps in a **new** `BokehModel` — new figures, new ranges, so the user's zoom/pan is gone and the stack visibly flashes. #127 removed every replot from the *selection* path but left this one on the *mode* path, where it is just as destructive.
- **Why it was there:** `_build_figures()` wired the two gestures conditionally — `brush_mode` decided whether a figure got a `BoxSelectTool` + `SelectionGeometry` handler or a `Tap` handler. Bokeh has no clean way to unregister an `on_event` callback, so re-wiring meant rebuilding. The fix removes the need to re-wire: **both** gestures are now registered on every figure at build time, and the modes differ only in which tool owns the click-drag.
- **`_apply_interaction_mode()`** walks the live figures (kept in the new `_figures` / `_box_tools` dicts) and sets `toolbar.active_drag` to that figure's `BoxSelectTool` in brush mode, or back to its `PanTool` (`_pan_tool()`, falling back to `"auto"`) in cutoff mode. Those are property writes on models the frontend already has, so the bars, the gate markers, the "Selected" overlay and the zoom all stay exactly as they were. `_build_figures()` ends with the same call, which keeps first-render behaviour — and the two existing `active_drag` tests (#112 reply) — unchanged.
- **Handlers became mode-aware, since they now fire in both modes.** `_make_tap_handler` returns early while brushing: Bokeh raises `Tap` for any click whatever the active tool is, and a bare click during a brush must not set a cutoff. `_make_range_handler` deliberately does *not* guard — a box-select gesture is unambiguous, so if the user activates the tool from the toolbar in cutoff mode, the drag gates a range rather than being silently dropped.
- `_render()` now clears `_figures` / `_box_tools` before its early returns, so a later mode switch cannot write to detached figures. `_on_bin_slider_change()` still replots, correctly — a different bin count is a different set of bars.
- Tests: new `TestHistogramInteractionModeSwitch` in `tests/test_histogram_plugin.py` (7 tests — a Brush→Cutoff→Brush round trip triggers zero `_render` calls; the figure objects are identical across the switch; `active_drag` flips to the figure's own box tool and back off it; both `SelectionGeometry` and `Tap` stay registered in either mode; a gate and its band survive the switch; a tap is ignored while brushing but honoured in cutoff mode). `python -m unittest discover -s tests -t .` → **882 tests, OK** (875 before). The gesture itself — dragging a range immediately after flipping the toggle, and the zoom actually surviving — needs a notebook check; the dev environment has no browser. See `dev_note/issue_tracking/issue127_histogram_gating.md`.

**Issue #129 — The histogram's "Main viewer" checkbox now gates every highlight it pushes**
- Reported as "when the Main viewer is not selected, the selected subset of cells in one histogram still affects the highlighting of the cells in the main viewer". Two independent paths, both in `ueler/viewer/plugin/histogram.py`.
- **The one that produced the outlines: `highlight_cells()` ended with `_apply_gate(publish=push_to_gallery, highlight=True)`** — `highlight=True` hard-coded, so it called `sync_mask_highlights_from_selection()` whatever the checkbox said. Brush mode was never affected (`handle_range` → `set_gate` → `_apply_gate(highlight=mv_linked_checkbox.value)`), which is why the bug looked intermittent: it reproduced in **Cutoff** interaction mode and not in **Brush**. The same unconditional path is reached by the above/below toggle (`_on_above_below_change`) and by `ImageMaskViewer._reapply_selection_highlights()`, which re-invokes `histogram_plugin.highlight_cells()` after *every* FOV change (`main_viewer.py:2409`) — so an unlinked histogram holding a cutoff re-drew its highlight on each FOV switch too. Fixed by reading the checkbox there as well; cutoff mode, the toggle and the FOV re-apply now behave exactly like a brush. This deliberately reverses the call recorded under #127 ("`highlight_cells()` keeps pushing mask highlights unconditionally … unifying those two policies would have changed cutoff behaviour for no benefit") — #129 *is* that benefit. The cell-gallery link is untouched: it has its own checkbox and its own `push_to_gallery` flag.
- **The one that kept them on screen: nothing observed the checkbox.** Unchecking stopped *updating* the highlight but left the outlines drawn while linked on the canvas, which from the user's side is indistinguishable from a live link. New `sync_main_viewer_link()` + `_on_mv_link_change`, wired in `_wire_events()`: unchecking clears through `sync_mask_highlights_from_selection(viewer, set())` — deliberately, since `set_mask_ids` calls `_forget_linked_selection()`, so that also drops the `linked_selection_indices` record (#119) and the next FOV switch cannot resurrect the highlight — and re-checking re-projects the current `selected_indices` onto the active FOV.
- **The observer is a no-op when this plugin has published no selection**, so toggling an idle histogram cannot wipe a highlight the scatter or heatmap plugin owns (`set_mask_ids` replaces the whole highlight; last writer wins). It also reads `mv_linked_checkbox.value` rather than the `change` payload, which keeps it independent of the observer's change object — the shared ipywidgets test stub ignores `observe` registrations entirely, so the tests invoke the handler directly and a separate test asserts the registration itself.
- Not changed: the scatter (`chart.py`) and heatmap (`heatmap_layers.py`) plugins already gate their highlight pushes on their own checkbox, but they share the second symptom (stale outlines after unlinking); that needs the same observer per plugin and is out of scope here.
- Tests: new `TestHistogramMainViewerLink` in `tests/test_histogram_plugin.py` (9 tests — a cutoff tap, an above/below flip, the FOV re-apply and a brush all leave the viewer untouched when unlinked; the cutoff highlights when linked; unchecking withdraws the outlines *and* the `linked_selection_indices` record; re-checking restores them; toggling without a selection touches nothing; the checkbox is observed). `_FakeImageDisplay` gained a `set_mask_ids_calls` counter — "never asked to highlight" and "asked to clear" both leave `last_mask_ids == []` — and now mirrors the real `ImageDisplay` by clearing the viewer's record. The two existing cutoff tests that assert on `image_display` opt into the link explicitly. `python -m unittest discover -s tests -t .` → **875 tests, OK** (866 before). The pointer gesture itself (tapping a histogram with the box unchecked) needs a notebook check; the dev environment has no browser. See `dev_note/issue_tracking/issue129_histogram_mv_link.md`.

**Issue #128 — Cell gallery tiles keep the requested size at the FOV border, so their scale is comparable**
- Reported as "images of cells close to the edge are cropped and do not maintain the original scale — their width is extended to fill the square". The stretch is real but the aspect ratio was never the problem: `render_crop_to_array()` **clamps** the requested box to the FOV (`_ensure_region_within_bounds`, `engine.py`), so a cell 10 px from the left border of a 40 px cutout comes back as a `40 × 30` array. The tile widget then draws every tile at the grid column width (`.tg-tile img { width: 100%; height: auto; }`, `tile_gallery_widget.py:138`), so those 30 columns are scaled up to the full width — a 1.33× zoom relative to an interior tile, with `height: auto` making the tile taller than a square. Each tile is independently fitted to the column, so what is lost is the **scale**, and comparing cell sizes across the gallery is exactly what that breaks.
- **Fix: pad, don't un-clamp.** `render_crop_to_array()` gained `pad_to_size` / `pad_color`; `_pad_region_to_requested_size()` blits the clamped array into a canvas of the size that was asked for. The clamp itself is kept — the annotation and mask overlays are sliced with the *same* clamped `region_ds` inside `render_fov_to_array()`, so widening the read region would have mis-registered every overlay. Padding after the render leaves that path untouched.
- **The pad sits where the missing data would have been**, not around a re-centred image: the offset is `region_ds[0] - requested_xmin // downsample` (and the row analogue), computed on the downsampled grid the array was itself sliced on, so the seam lands on a whole output pixel and the cell stays put inside the tile. Offsets and the blit extent are clamped, so the output is exactly square even when `ceil()` rounding at `downsample_factor > 1` disagrees with the region arithmetic by a pixel.
- **Opt-in, deliberately.** The batch-export single-cell paths re-derive the clamped region themselves with `compute_crop_regions()` and hand the array to `apply_overlay_snapshot_to_array()`; silently changing the array's shape under them would break that registration. So `pad_to_size` defaults to `False` and only the cell gallery passes `pad_to_size=True, pad_color=GALLERY_PAD_COLOR`. Export still writes short crops for edge cells — a known follow-up, noted in the tracking document.
- **White padding**, per the issue. Against the black composite background it reads unambiguously as "outside the field of view" rather than as signal, and it is a parameter rather than a constant in the renderer so it can be changed without touching `engine.py`.
- Side effect worth noting: `_compose_canvas()` (the legacy Matplotlib composer) centre-pads variable tile sizes with black, so it showed the same cells at the wrong scale without the stretch. Now that every tile leaves the renderer at the same size, its padding branch is a no-op.
- Tests: `CropPaddingTests` in `tests/test_rendering.py` (padding at each edge and at a corner, pad colour, real pixels preserved at the right offset, no-op for interior crops, the unpadded default kept for the export path, square output at `downsample_factor > 1`) and `TestGalleryEdgeTileScale` in `tests/test_cell_gallery.py` (every tile square at the requested size, edge tile matches interior tile, out-of-FOV area padded rather than stretched, holds at a downsample factor). Full suite: **866 tests, OK**.

### v0.4.3
**Issue #127 — The histogram gates on several channels at once, and each histogram keeps its own term**
- Asked after establishing that a histogram selection was *not* an intersection: `HistogramDisplay` held exactly one term, in two mutually exclusive forms — `_brush_selection = (channel, lo, hi)`, which `handle_range()` **overwrote** on every gesture, and `cutoff` + `_active_histogram_column`, which `highlight_cells()` read alone. Brushing CD4 then CD8 selected "cells in the CD8 range"; a range and a cutoff could not be combined at all. What made it easy to misread was `_refresh_overlays()`, which redraws the single selection on *every* figure — linked **display**, not linked filtering.
- **Fix: one term per channel, ANDed.** `_gates: dict[channel, (kind, a, b)]` replaces the tuple, where a term is `("range", lo, hi)` or `("cutoff", direction, value)`; `gated_indices()` builds one boolean mask per term and `&`s them. With a single term the result is bit-for-bit the old behaviour, which is what keeps single-channel use unchanged. A term naming a column absent from the frame is **skipped**, not treated as empty — otherwise a gate would silently select nothing whenever the plotted subset dropped a column.
- **The cutoff is a first-class gate term now.** `highlight_cells()` folds the pending `cutoff`/`_active_histogram_column` pair into `_gates` and captures the above/below direction *at that moment*, so each channel keeps its own direction and the `Highlight:` toggle re-applies only the last cutoff's channel. A cutoff on CD4 therefore intersects a brushed range on CD8. The pair itself was kept rather than folded away because the viewer re-triggers `histogram_plugin.highlight_cells()` on a FOV change (`main_viewer.py:2408`) and callers assign them directly.
- **Both modes now measure the same thing.** A brush filtered `_plot_data` (subset-filtered, NaN-dropped) while a cutoff filtered `main_viewer.cell_table` directly, so the same threshold meant different things depending on which mode set it. `_gate_frame()` returns `_plot_data` when a plot exists and the full table otherwise — so a cutoff respects the **Subset** tab (the one intentional behaviour change), while a cutoff set before any plot, or re-applied on a FOV change, still has the table to fall back on. `highlight_cells()` deliberately keeps pushing mask highlights *unconditionally* (it is the explicit "show the gate in the viewer" entry point the viewer itself calls) while a brush still highlights only when **Main viewer** is linked; unifying those two policies would have changed cutoff behaviour for no benefit here.
- **Per-histogram state was the other half of the request: "don't refresh on selection".** Two things used to erase the earlier channel's state on sight — Bokeh's box-select overlay is transient and only marks the figure being dragged, and its (non)selection glyphs grey out the non-selected bars of that figure. So each figure now owns a `BoxAnnotation` (its range band, in `sources[channel]["band"]`) and a `Span` (its cutoff line), both *ours* and both persistent, and `selection_glyph`/`nonselection_glyph` are pinned to the base glyph so a drag changes nothing visually. `_apply_gate()` is deliberately narrow: it publishes `selected_indices`, optionally syncs mask highlights, and rewrites only the overlay sources plus the annotations — it never calls `_render()`/`plot_histograms()`, so a selection cannot rebuild the stack or lose a zoom/pan (cf. #109, #119). A test asserts `_render` is not called across a brush, a cutoff, a per-channel clear and a full clear.
- **The cross-channel overlay still refreshes, on purpose.** `_refresh_overlays()` touches only each figure's `selected` ColumnDataSource — no figures, axes or bin edges — so it cannot disturb a zoom or another channel's marker, and with gating it is what shows the gated population's distribution in every channel. "Don't refresh on selection" is honoured where a refresh is destructive (replots, markers, zoom), not for a source update that cannot disturb anything.
- **Clearing became per-channel.** Double-tap a histogram → `clear_gate(channel)` drops just that term and re-applies the rest (registered in both modes). `Clear selection` drops every term *and* the cutoff state — it previously cleared only `_brush_selection`, leaving a live cutoff behind. A replot drops terms whose channel is no longer plotted and re-publishes if any were; `show_external_selection()` replaces the gate rather than intersecting with it, since those indices come from another plugin's criteria and stale terms would misrepresent them.
- Rejected: a `Replace`/`Intersect` mode toggle. A single brush behaves identically either way — the modes only diverge once a second channel is gated, which is the feature — and a toggle would need persisted state plus a third path through `_apply_gate`. Per-channel clearing covers the "only this channel" case without a mode. Added instead: `gate_description()` → a `gate_summary` label reading e.g. `Gate: CD4 ∈ [0.2, 0.9] AND CD8 > 0.5`, because a gate spread over several figures (one possibly scrolled out of view) is otherwise invisible.
- Files: `ueler/viewer/plugin/histogram.py` only; `_refresh_cutoff_spans()` and `_on_brush()` are kept as aliases and `_brush_selection` is gone (nothing outside the module referenced it). Tests: new `TestHistogramGating` in `tests/test_histogram_plugin.py` (19 tests — range ∩ range, cutoff ∩ cutoff, range ∩ cutoff, re-acting replaces only that channel's term, a brush superseding a cutoff on the same channel, the toggle flipping only the last cutoff, per-channel vs. full clear, a replot dropping a term, external selection replacing the gate, the no-replot guard, the subset-vs-full-table frame, and the description text), plus the persistent-band and glyph-pinning tests; `test_cutoff_span_shows_only_on_active_channel` became `..._on_gated_channels`, since spans now show on **every** gated channel by design. `python -m unittest discover -s tests -t .` → **853 tests, OK** (832 before). The pointer gestures themselves — double-tap to clear, the band mid-drag — need a notebook check; the dev environment has no browser. See `dev_note/issue_tracking/issue127_histogram_gating.md`.

**Issue #119 (follow-up) — The highlight also survives a zoom and a pan**
- Reported after the FOV-switch fix below: the selected cells were highlighted after switching FOV, but "not persistent after zooming in and out — first highlighted, then cleared after a few seconds. Either they are really cleared or they are overwritten by a mask update." The second guess is right: **overwritten**. A zoom never touches the selection cache; `selected_masks_label` still holds the correct triples for the (unchanged) FOV.
- **Root cause: the outline is not a Matplotlib artist.** `ImageDisplay.update_patches` paints white pixels *into* a copy of the RGB array and pushes it with `set_data`, so any later `set_data` of a freshly rendered array erases it — which is exactly the order `update_display` used: it called `update_patches()` inside the mask branch and only *then* ran `img_display.set_data(combined)`. A zoom or pan changes the axis limits → `draw_event` → `ImageDisplay.on_draw` → `update_display(...)`, so step one outlined the **pre-zoom** array (the frame the report saw as "first highlighted") and step two replaced it with the new render. This is the latent ordering bug that was noted and deliberately left alone in the first #119 pass; it never affected the FOV switch because `_reapply_selection_highlights()` runs *after* `update_display` returns.
- **A second consequence, also fixed by the same reorder:** `update_patches` compares its expected region size against `combined.shape`, which at that point was still the previous viewport's array — so it could fall back to the absolute-offset mapping (`rows + ymin_ds`) and place the outline outside the visible slice even in the one frame where it was drawn.
- **Fix: repaint after installing the new array.** The mask branch now only records that a repaint is due; `update_patches()` runs after `set_data(combined)` / `image_display.combined = combined` / `set_extent(...)`. `combined` is the **clean** render, so outlines are never baked into the base image and cannot accumulate — `update_patches` re-derives them from `combined` every time. The flag is set only in the single-FOV mask branch, so map mode keeps its own path (`update_patches` there just delegates to `_update_map_mask_highlights()`, which `update_display` already calls itself).
- Left alone deliberately: the dead `if getattr(self, '_in_on_draw', False): return` guard at the top of `update_patches` — nothing ever sets `_in_on_draw`, and making it live would suppress exactly the repaint this fix relies on. Also `MaskPainter.on_mv_update_display` → `set_mask_colors_current_fov`, which rebuilds from `combined` and would wipe the outline, but fires only on a FOV / identifier / continuous-spec change (never on a plain zoom) and each of those paths re-highlights afterwards.
- Files: `ueler/viewer/main_viewer.py`. Tests: new `tests/test_issue119_zoom_highlight.py` (12 tests) runs the real `update_display` *and* the real `update_patches` and asserts on the **pixels of the array left on screen** — the ordering bug is invisible to a test that only inspects `selected_masks_label`. Covered: the last-installed array carries the outline, it traces the selected cell and not the unselected one, `combined` stays outline-free, a zoom-in → zoom-out sequence keeps the highlight at every step, the outline is aligned to the new viewport, a coarser downsample factor still works, plus a negative control (a viewport entirely inside a cell correctly shows nothing) and the unchanged map-mode / no-selection / suspended-update paths. Confirmed as a genuine regression test: with the old ordering restored, 8 of the 12 fail. `python -m unittest discover -s tests -t .` → **833 tests, OK** (821 before). See `dev_note/issue_tracking/issue119_selection_across_fov.md`.

**Issue #119 — A plot selection stays highlighted after switching FOV**
- The hypothesis in the issue was exactly right: the highlight was cached in a form that only described the FOV that was active when the selection was made. A plot selection starts as a set of **cell-table row indices** — FOV-independent, owned by the plugin (`ChartDisplay.selected_indices`, `HistogramDisplay.selected_indices`). `_chart_common.sync_mask_highlights_from_selection` then *projects* those rows onto the active FOV and discards the rest (`rows.loc[rows[fov_col] == active_fov, lbl_col]`), `ImageDisplay.set_mask_ids` narrows further to ids that exist in the current FOV's label mask and tags each with that FOV, and `update_patches` draws only `sel.fov == current_fov`. After a switch the cache described the *previous* FOV, the filter matched nothing, and no outlines were drawn — while the plugin still held the correct selection that nobody asked it to re-project.
- **Why the histogram cutoff and the linked heatmap already survived a switch, and nothing else did.** `on_image_change` discarded what was left (`image_display.clear_patches()`) and then re-invoked exactly two things: `histogram_output.highlight_cells()` (the **cutoff** highlight — needs a cutoff set, and recomputes from the active FOV) and, when linked, `heatmap_output.highlight_cells()` (the cluster highlight). Both recompute per FOV, so both looked fine. A scatter lasso/box selection and a histogram **brush** are row-index selections and had no such path — which is the pair named in the issue.
- **Fix: remember the selection in the form that survives, and re-project it.** The viewer gains `linked_selection_indices`, written by `sync_mask_highlights_from_selection` (the single function every row-index selection already funnels through, so no plugin needed changing) and re-projected by a new `ImageMaskViewer._reapply_selection_highlights()` from `on_image_change`. The per-FOV cache itself is untouched, so nothing else that reads `selected_masks_label` — `trace_cells`, `trace_cluster`, the cell-table editor, the exporters — sees a different shape. Storing all FOVs' triples at once instead (dropping the per-FOV filter) was rejected for exactly that reason, plus growth by the FOV count on a whole-dataset selection.
- **Last-writer-wins is preserved by invalidating the record in `set_mask_ids`.** Three plugins can drive the highlight and `set_mask_ids` replaces the whole set, so only one selection is ever on screen; `sync_...` re-arms the record right after its own `set_mask_ids` call, while the cutoff/cluster `highlight_cells` methods that call it directly clear it and keep their own per-FOV recomputation. A click, ctrl-click, lasso or `clear_patches` clears it too — those are spatial, per-FOV selections and must not resurrect a stale plot selection on the next switch. This is also why a single viewer-side record was chosen over an `on_fov_change` hook per plugin: three hooks would clobber each other in `dir(SidePlots)` order.
- **The change is additive.** The re-projection branch is taken only for a non-empty record; otherwise the previous `clear_patches` + cutoff + cluster block runs verbatim, so no existing highlight behaviour is altered. Clearing a plot selection empties it through `set_mask_ids([])`, which drops the record — "clear, then switch FOV" behaves as before rather than restoring something the user just dismissed. The re-projection also runs at the same point in `on_image_change` as the block it replaces, because `update_display` overwrites the canvas from `self.combined` and would wipe outlines drawn before it.
- Files: `ueler/viewer/main_viewer.py` (the record + `_reapply_selection_highlights`), `ueler/viewer/plugin/_chart_common.py` (write the record), `ueler/viewer/image_display.py` (new `_forget_linked_selection`, called from `set_mask_ids` / `clear_patches` / `on_mouse_click` / `_on_lasso_selected`).
- Tests: new `tests/test_issue119_selection_across_fov.py` (17 tests, driving the real `set_mask_ids` / `sync_mask_highlights_from_selection` / `_reapply_selection_highlights` against a two-FOV table whose labels are reused in both FOVs, so an un-reprojected highlight cannot accidentally look right — the round trip FOV1→FOV2→FOV1, a FOV with no selected cells, the legacy fallback including the `main_viewer_checkbox` gate, every invalidation path, and map mode). `python -m unittest discover -s tests -t .` → **821 tests, OK** (804 before). Whether Matplotlib actually paints the outlines after the switch needs a notebook check. Noted in passing and left alone: `update_display` calls `update_patches()` *before* `img_display.set_data(combined)`, so those outlines are painted onto the array that is about to be replaced — a real latent bug on a different path, worth its own issue. See `dev_note/issue_tracking/issue119_selection_across_fov.md`.

**Issue #126 — The selected-channel chips are drag-reorderable again**
- Not a broken feature but one that was never carried over. ipywidgets' `TagsInput` implemented drag-reordering inside its own view; `ChannelPickerWidget`, the anywidget that replaced it for #125, renders its chips itself and gave them only a label and a `×` — no `draggable`, no drag listeners. So the only way to change the order was to clear the selection and re-pick every channel in the desired sequence.
- **The order is not cosmetic**, which is why this matters: `main_viewer.update_controls` iterates `channel_selector.value` and assigns `channel_controls_box.children` in exactly that order (the per-channel visibility/name/colour header and the Min/Max contrast sliders), and channel compositing in `_compose_fov_image` follows it too. That also means the issue's second requirement — "the order should be reflected in the channel color and scale UI" — needed **no new wiring**: committing a permuted `value` reaches `update_controls` through the `names='value'` observer that was already registered. And because `update_controls` reuses the per-channel widget objects keyed by channel name, a reorder moves the rows **without** resetting the user's colour choices or contrast settings (asserted by a test).
- **Fix, entirely inside the widget's ESM/CSS.** Each chip becomes a drag source — a `⋮⋮` grip as the affordance, `cursor: grab`, `draggable`, and `dragstart`/`dragover`/`dragleave`/`drop`/`dragend`. The whole chip is the handle rather than a grip-only hit area (an 11px chip has little room to spare), with the `×` opted out (`draggable = false` plus a `dragstart` that stops propagation) so removing a chip stays a click and not an aborted drag. `dragover` compares `e.clientX` against the target chip's horizontal midpoint and shows a `drop-before`/`drop-after` indicator, so both sides of every chip are reachable and a chip can reach either end of the row in one gesture; dropping in the empty space past the last chip appends, via a container-level handler that the per-chip handlers `stopPropagation()` out of. The indicator is an absolutely-positioned pseudo-element drawn in the 3px inter-chip gap rather than a border or margin — a marker that changed the chip's box would reflow the row mid-drag and move the drop target out from under the pointer. `user-select: none` keeps a drag from turning into a text selection.
- **The one piece of real logic is isolated and executed by the tests.** `reorderSelection(selection, name, target, after)` sits at ESM module scope between marker comments with no DOM access, and resolves the target index **after** splicing the source out. Resolving it first — as `mask_class_list_widget`'s row reorder does — lands every left-to-right move one slot too far, because removing the source shifts the target left; the test for that case records the wrong answer alongside the right one. The tests extract the function from `_ESM` and run it under `node`, so what is asserted is the code the browser executes rather than a Python re-implementation that could drift, and `node --check` parses the whole ESM (a syntax error there breaks every picker at render time and nothing else would catch it — the ESM is an opaque string to Python and the headless test fallback never evaluates it).
- **A keyboard path was added alongside the drag.** Chips are `tabIndex = 0` with `role="listitem"` and an `aria-label`; ← / → move the focused chip, Delete/Backspace removes it, and focus follows the chip to its new position by name (the commit re-renders, so the element identity changes). HTML5 drag-and-drop never fires for touch input and some notebook hosts intercept drag events before a widget sees them, so this keeps reordering reachable there — and usable without a mouse.
- No file outside `ueler/viewer/plugin/channel_picker_widget.py` changed. All five pickers built by `build_channel_picker` inherit the behaviour — the left-panel **Channels** selector plus the Scatter plot, Histogram, Heatmap and FlowSOM marker/feature pickers — which is right, since marker order is meaningful in those plots too. The option list itself stays in `allowed_tags` order (markers first for an AnnData table, per #123) and is deliberately not user-orderable.
- Tests: new `tests/test_issue126_chip_reorder.py` (26 tests — the node-executed drop arithmetic across both drag directions × both drop sides, append, first↔last, self-drop, unknown name, empty selection, and an exhaustive `4 × 5 × 2` sweep asserting no channel is ever duplicated or lost; the ESM syntax check; the front-end contract; a permutation surviving `_normalise_selection` and firing its observers; and `update_controls` reordering the colour/contrast rows while reusing the slider objects). `python -m unittest discover -s tests -t .` → **804 tests, OK** (778 before). The pointer gesture itself is unverified — the dev environment has no browser (no playwright/chromium) — so drag, the grip's appearance and the indicator's placement should be confirmed in a notebook. See `dev_note/issue_tracking/issue126_chip_drag_reorder.md`.

**Issue #123 — AnnData is accepted as a cell-table input**
- `load_cell_table` only took a pandas DataFrame or a CSV path. An AnnData object passed through `set_cell_table` unvalidated (that method was literally `self.cell_table = cell_table`) and then failed at the first plugin that touched the table, so users flattened by hand — `script/run_ueler_MCB.ipynb` still does exactly that (`read_h5ad` → rename `cell_id`→`label` → `pd.merge` into the CSV table). Both an AnnData object and an `.h5ad` path are now accepted: `load_cell_table(viewer, cell_table=adata)` / `load_cell_table(viewer, cell_table_path='cells.h5ad')`, plus `layer=` to read a `layers` entry instead of `X` and `obsm_keys=` to opt wide `obsm` entries in.
- **Design: keep the AnnData, derive a DataFrame view of it.** `viewer.cell_table` is read at ~108 sites across 15 plugin modules, all assuming pandas (`.loc`/`.iloc`/`.index`/`select_dtypes`/`groupby().median()`/`.iterrows()`/in-place `drop`/column assignment) — and two write paths call pandas *free functions* on the object (`pd.merge(cell_table, …)` in `heatmap_layers.save_to_cell_table`, `df.query(…)` in `export_fovs.refresh_cell_options`), which an adapter class could not satisfy without subclassing `DataFrame`. So the user's object is retained on `viewer.cell_table_adata` and `viewer.cell_table` becomes a pandas view of it: `obs` columns, one column per `var_names` entry, narrow `obsm` arrays as `X_umap1`/`X_umap2`, and `obs_names` as a plain column. `viewer.cell_table_columns` records which columns came from where. Marker columns keep their `float32` dtype, so the view costs one copy of `X` rather than a float64 doubling. Every DataFrame code path is unchanged — both new attributes stay `None` for a DataFrame or CSV table.
- **The view gets a `RangeIndex`, and `obs_names` is kept as a column.** The plugins mix label-based access (`idx in cell_table.index`, `_chart_common.sync_mask_highlights_from_selection`) with *positional* access (`df.iloc[cell_index]`, `cell_gallery`); those agree only on a `RangeIndex`, which is what `pd.read_csv` has always produced. Keeping AnnData's string `obs_names` as the index would have made lasso→gallery selection silently select nothing. Re-indexing is positional rather than a concat on `obs_names`, since AnnData tolerates duplicate names.
- **(code) `category` obs columns hid every class column in the UI.** `write_h5ad` calls `strings_to_categoricals`, so a re-read `obs` returns `category` for every string column — and `select_dtypes(include=['int', 'int64', 'object'])`, the rule behind eight dropdown-population sites, returns **only** `['label']` for such a table. `fov` and `cell_type` disappeared from the cluster, subset-on, mask-painter identifier and tooltip-label dropdowns entirely. The eight sites (`annotation_display.py`, `heatmap.py`, `heatmap_layers.py` ×2, `run_flowsom.py` ×2, `mask_painter.py`) now share a new `ueler.cell_table.categorical_columns()` that accepts `category`/`string` too, and `_chart_common.build_subset_controls` (whose `is_numeric_dtype or is_object_dtype` test had the same gap) was widened. Behaviour for a CSV table is unchanged — `read_csv` never yields `category`, and `bool` stays opt-in because only the mask painter included it. A test asserts the CSV-path result is exactly the pre-refactor list.
- **(code) `category` obs columns broke manual labelling.** `Categorical` rejects assignment of a value outside its categories — `TypeError: Cannot setitem on a Categorical with a new category (Z)` — which is precisely `ct.loc[mask, column_name] = value` in the Cell Table Editor, so faithfully preserving the dtype would have made the editor crash on any `.h5ad` table. `flatten_anndata` therefore widens `category` columns: numeric categories become a real numeric column, everything else `object`, so the derived frame behaves exactly like a CSV-loaded one. `write_h5ad` re-categorises strings on the way out, so the round-trip is not lossy. (Found by exercising real `anndata` 0.11.4 / `pandas` 2.3.3, not by inspection — both defects only appear after an `.h5ad` round-trip, which is how most users will arrive.)
